Beck: So is that stat, is that all adult American women of any age? Beck: I’m still really—I’m still really surprised by that. So is it that the trend is increasing, but overall it’s still just a really small percentage of people, or what’s the deal? Gilbert: It is increasing. It’s not increasing by that much. From what I can figure out, I think Botox usage has gone up 50 percent over the last 10 years, which is not really that much considering how much it went up in the 2000s when it first was popularized in our culture. And I should say, too, that it’s expensive, so it’s very much a sort of— Gilbert: —selective expense, and there are lots of people who can’t afford it and might want it but don’t have that in their budget. But it is, yeah, it’s a fascinating statistic. Beck: Something I’m noticing in this specific moment is that this newfound emphasis on body modification feels hard to talk about. Because it’s like we have held on to one value from the body-positivity era, which is you can talk about your own body, but don’t comment on other people’s bodies. So are we stuck in that place of having a conversation with old language, or what’s going on here? Gilbert: Yeah, it’s really difficult, right? I think because we’re sort of traumatized from this moment where there was so much relentless critique and obsessive, like, nitpicky focus on women’s bodies in the public eye, I think we’ve rightfully have recognized as extremely damaging, and we do not want to go back to that place. But it does kind of maroon us now in a place where—there’s also something about the way that women are appearing in public at the moment. We’ve seen a lot more images recently of very, very, very slender celebrities. I would say past the point of slender, even. Gilbert: That is making us uncomfortable, and we don’t really have ways to describe it without—’cause obviously, we’re not wanting to criticize anyone for their body, because we know how damaging that can be. But also, what is really troubling to me is the cultural impact. I think there are ways maybe to critique it as a cultural trend and not without picking on anyone individually, but it’s one of the things—this sort of backlash from the 2000s—is one of the things that makes this moment so hard to talk about. Beck: Right, it’s a really hard conversation to have. Like, even right now as we’re talking about it, I’m worried people are going to think we’re saying, Actually we should be judging and commenting on other people’s bodies more, which is not what we’re saying. But when there’s a big cultural shift toward valuing a certain type of look, when enough people are making similar choices, that does affect how people feel about their own bodies. And then if you shut down that bigger conversation by saying, We shouldn’t talk about this at all, because it’s not appropriate to comment on other people’s bodies, and everyone should just do whatever they want, then we lose the opportunity to question this pretty damaging new collective beauty standard that is developing. For instance, the rise of GLP-1s: It’s led to this new era of “thin is in” again. Gilbert: I think what you just said is really interesting with regard to GLP-1s, right? Because they’ve been really revolutionary medical treatments for lots of people. Gilbert: I have family members on them who have just had the most profound experiences, whose health has been transformed at an individual level. They’re enormously beneficial and helpful and positive for so many people. And speaking of statistics, I think one in eight Americans now is on a GLP-1, so much, much higher uptake than Botox. But we live in a society, and more than one thing can be true at the same time, right? Something can be positive at an individual level and it can also have really damaging cultural ramifications. There has been such a tendency towards extreme thinness that has accompanied this kind of Ozempic era. And that has a potentially damaging impact on the kids, the girls and the boys who are growing up in this era, and holding that up as a standard to aspire to, because it’s an impossible standard, and it’s not healthy, and it’s something that I think we need to find ways to call out while also acknowledging that everyone does have an individual choice to do whatever they need to do for their health. Beck: I think the issue for me feels like the separation of your individual choice from, like, the stew that you live in. ’Cause even if you’re not a celebrity or an influencer, your choices do still influence your friends and your family and people who see you and know you. Gilbert: I think one way that is maybe productive, or one thing that is maybe productive for people to think about is that we don’t have to opt in to these beauty standards, to these regimes, to these sort of, like, money-making enterprises. And in some ways, I think we should celebrate opting out as a form of rebellion. Like, the most radical thing you can do right now is just accept yourself exactly as you are, to refuse to have the sort of self-obsession of constantly critiquing yourself and wondering how you could change yourself. I think Naomi Wolf wrote about this in The Beauty Myth, which, you know, she’s gone wayward since then, but I think about it all the time. It’s this idea that this is all a project of distraction, especially for women. Gilbert: Like, when we’re so focused on keeping ourselves small, it seems so conservative in some ways. If you keep women completely obsessed with themselves and the project of self-maintenance, they’re not thinking about what else they actually need or how to change the world or what they would do if they had power to organize or to demand changes at their workplace or in their homes. It’s a big, big project of distraction. And so the more that we can refuse to be swayed by it, the more that we can embrace sort of visibly aging, visibly being unkempt as a sort of, actually quite a profound choice and one that is meaningful and has meaning for lots of other women as well, not just for us, then maybe the more we can kick-start a sort of, a pushback against this movement. Beck: Yeah. You used the word distraction, and that stood out to me because I feel like we have the greatest technologies of distraction of all time, in our pockets all the time. So maybe it makes sense that there would be an unholy alliance between the distraction of beauty standards and the distraction of the algorithm. Gilbert: There is definitely a growing, actively misogynist movement on the right that, for example, does want women to opt out of the workplace and to be completely entrenched in the domestic sphere. And it’s no coincidence to me that so many of the visible avatars for that movement who are women are so groomed. And the idea of clean-girl beauty and stuff like that, it’s all part and parcel of the same thing, which is like, This is what you should be thinking about, ladies. Don’t worry about the other stuff. We’ll take care of that. Beck: Well, and it’s also—it makes people money, right? Beck: It makes the beauty companies money, and it makes the tech companies money if you keep, like, scroll, scroll, scrolling. Gilbert: Anytime anyone gets that rich off of making other people feel bad about themselves, I think we should raise an eyebrow. Beck: You know, for all of their faults, which have been enumerated many times, earlier versions of social media were at least, like, a little bit more collective, a little bit more about connection, right? Beck: For instance, Instagram used to show you your friends’ posts in chronological order. Beck: And now it shows you individually targeted videos from accounts you never chose to follow, and your actual friends just get, like, buried in there. Beck: So to me, it feels like less of a place to connect and more like a place to consume. Gilbert: It’s more of a place to be sold things. Instagram is a mall now, I think. It’s whatever can compel us to click the “Buy” button. You know, 10 years ago, there was a lot more text-based social media.There were sites like Tumblr. Gilbert: People would write long, long Facebook posts that were just text and didn’t have images or video attached. I mean, it’s that the sites—the social-media sites—have just become so, so, so, so image-heavy. It is very, very gendered and it’s very, very focused on getting us to buy things. There’s a great quote by John Berger in his 1977 book, Ways of Seeing, about why beauty ads are damaging, and he says something like: The beauty ad steals the woman’s love of herself as she is and sells it back to her for the price of the product. Which is so much of what’s going on. It’s this idea that we cannot be allowed to love ourselves as we are, because if we do, we simply will not buy enough. Beck: Do you have any kind of advice for how to deal with this? Gilbert: I think logging off can be profoundly effective, like having a digital detox, removing ourselves from these sort of fake people who feel like they’re real but are not. And spending more time face-to-face with real people, talking to them, and having sort of broader concerns and broader interests. It’s very strange to be saturated in visual perfection all the time. And when you go out in the real world and you see what people actually look like, it’s different. Sounds so trite and so basic, but it can be really powerful. Beck: What do you think that an individualistic social-media environment does to our ability to see collective problems or come up with collective solutions? Gilbert: We’ve been living in an individualistic culture for sort of 30 years now. Basically, the moment that third-wave feminism, which was so much about, like, improving on second-wave feminism to make it more inclusive and more successful for other women, and thinking collectively and not just think—as soon as that was replaced by post-feminism and this idea that, like, Any woman can do anything she wants, ’cause that’s her choice, and she can make money and wear whatever she wants and have sex with anyone she wants.The minute that that shift happened, we lost, I think, all the force of—I’m speaking about feminism, but it speaks broadly to other kinds of activism and progress as well. You simply cannot change the world if you are only thinking about yourself. Again, it comes back to this project of distraction and the idea that we should all be so focused on ourselves and the project of ourselves, and how to have our revenue streams and, our side hustles and our five-to-nines. And because if we do that, we’re not actually thinking about other people. We’re not going to community board meetings or school meetings or PTA meetings. We’re losing that sense of looking beyond ourselves. I think we need so much more of that, and I hope, I hope that it comes soon. Beck: Is the solution to just get offline and touch grass, or to change the culture of the internet, or a little bit of both? Gilbert: A little bit of both, but I would say, actively remembering the force that body positivity had in the 2010s and what it was able to do and the real impact that it had in the culture, and to sort of remember that that’s possible, I think, and that it can be done again. To remember that online lives are not real, and online beauty standards are not real. The thing I keep coming back to is, again, just like divorcing this idea that our value as human beings is how we look. There’s so much that’s liberating when you learn to let that go, and it does get easier as you get older and older; I can tell you from 43. But anything that we can do to reclaim our love for ourselves and our acceptance of ourselves and our sense of value as humans is profoundly positive. Beck: Natalie, Sophie used a phrase that I’ve heard a lot, especially since starting to work on this podcast. The internet is not real. And I know what she means to say, but I kind of have a problem with that way of thinking about things. Beck: Well, the internet is real. (Laughs.) It really exists. There are real tubes under the ocean that carry data. There are massive data centers that exist in physical space, and they use up real energy. You know, I have real conversations on the internet with real people who really exist. Beck: And the things people look at online do have real effects on their lives, on their mental health, and even potentially on their bodies, as we were just talking about. Brennan: Yeah. I think the spirit of The internet isn’t real is trying to connote that there’s this alternative, right? That you can opt out and embrace that which is offline. I think it’s just trying to communicate attention that a lot of us are trying to find separation from. I saw this quote from Haley Nahman on Substack recently that said, “The mirror worlds of social media and Hollywood and women’s media may present our participation as inevitable, but this is effectively an advertisement for a future that doesn’t have to exist.” And I think what people are feeling right now is that they wanna push back against that inevitability. They want to create an alternative future. You know, my mom said something to this extent. “Maybe it’s not about what is versus what isn’t inherently real, but about choosing what reality you want to exist in.” Mom: Your reality has to be centered inside of you, not outside of you. That’s a screen, and that screen isn’t telling you the real story of who you are. Right? And listen, I mean, even as a 60-something mom, I still fall into the trap of seeing things on my Instagram feed that make me think, Oh, maybe I should be doing that, or maybe I should … You know? It is so easy to be influenced, but then you just always have to come back to, like, I have to be who I am. I have to live in my own world. But you know what? It’s all in how people are talking to one another and how you’re talking to your children. And so all of that still matters. Talking matters. Brennan: Yeah. Talking def matters. Well, thanks for talking with me today. Beck: That’s all for this episode of How to Touch Grass. Summer he was 9 going on 10, suddenly he had one: chin tucked into the leading shoulder, the body at once lifted high and crouched; something in the thighs was attached to the ground yet there was an upward determination in the elbow— a boy could be pulled skyward and earthward at once, could be, like the bat, expectant, not quite still, quivering with intent to circle and strike a note; his hands fused to the wood and wrists braced as though screwing tight or about to unscrew the lid of a jar (love in the wrists was part of the stance) and when the swing broke and the ball whistled long and everyone was moving, calling—the throw’s to second— I wanted to study the stance again in that suspended life before a play happens, before memory carried it away. The Atlantic
France’s Best Argument Against Racism Isn’t Working
2026-08-09

France’s Best Argument Against Racism Isn’t Working

The Atlantic | 2026-08-09

Visit Original Website
Last month, the French striker Kylian Mbappé recorded the 22nd goal of his World Cup career during the bronze-medal match, establishing himself, at the age of 27, as the greatest scorer in tournament history. Even though the French national team, Les Bleus, failed to win the championship, Mbappé has become one of the country’s two most celebrated figures. At the final the next day, the camera panned across the audience and easily found the other one: Victor Wembanyama, the 22-year-old, 7-foot-4 basketball player who is widely considered to be the future of the NBA. Their success points to a stark irony: France’s most beloved men reflect a demographic shift that a growing share of their country rejects. Born in France, Mbappé and Wembanyama are the sons of African fathers; Mbappé’s mother is of Algerian descent; and many members of Les Bleus are Black and French Arab. As they’ve become more popular, so has the far-right party National Rally, or RN, which has promised to curtail immigration and promoted a racially restrictive notion of Frenchness that would seem to exclude Mbappé and Wembanyama. Along with soccer stars such as Ousmane Dembélé and Michael Olise, Wembanyama and Mbappé are living rebukes to the far right’s ideology of ethnic resentment. Indeed, they are the nation’s most compelling argument against it—an accomplished, elegant, and omnipresent representation of a multicultural France. But their argument is losing: After decades outside the political mainstream, the RN seems well positioned to win the presidency next year. As France shows, cultural representation won’t change politics. That requires something much more. France won its first World Cup in 1998, fielding a diverse team whose mass appeal briefly suggested that the country would embrace its multiracial future. The slogan “Black, blanc, beur” (“Black, white, Arab”), a play on the Tricolor, became shorthand for the idea that France’s racial identity had broadened. Jean-Marie Le Pen, the founder of the party that became the RN, had deemed it “artificial to bring in players from abroad and call them the French national team,” and claimed that they couldn’t sing “La Marseillaise.” But while the far right commanded a substantial protest vote at the time, it was still largely denied legitimate governing power. Sally Jenkins: When France plays soccer, you can’t look away Over the next two decades, the country grew more diverse as a result of greater immigration, particularly from Africa. Riots and terrorist attacks prompted debates about France’s identity and Islam’s place within it. Meanwhile, the RN became more viable at the polls. After Les Bleus lost in the 2010 World Cup, Le Pen’s daughter Marine—who would be the runner-up in the 2017 presidential election—accused some players of having “another nationality at heart.” A few years later, she called them “a bunch of badly brought up boys.” Then, in 2018, the French won the World Cup again. I watched the final in Paris at one of the many cafés that set up flatscreens along their terraces. Compared with Les Bleus of 1998, this version looked less like a liberal aspiration than a genuine reflection of France’s demographics. During the game, my two-week-old son slept beside me, a new French American with seawater eyes and African ancestors who’d been sold as slaves in territories that were later absorbed into France’s sprawling network of colonies. (It is a point of great familial pride that his maternal great-grandfather, Marcel Pinel, played for France in the first World Cup, in 1930.) Mbappé, then 19, became the second-youngest player in history to score a goal in a final, putting Les Bleus up three. As the game ended, a multihued crowd erupted around us. White French pensioners embraced Muslim teens whom they might have crossed the street to avoid in another context. I have only ever witnessed such color-blind fraternity on one other occasion: the night in 2008 that Barack Obama was elected president of the United States. In hindsight, both moments show that lasting political progress requires more than fleeting euphoria. Still, France’s win seemed to matter, at least for a while. One illustration of this came soon after the victory when the comedian Trevor Noah joked that “Africa won the World Cup.” For Noah, this was cause for celebration. But the quip provoked a broad backlash because it echoed the far right’s complaint that the team was “not French enough.” Gérard Araud, the French ambassador to the U.S., said that Noah’s joke “legitimizes” racism. “Unlike the United States of America, France does not refer to their citizens based on their race, religion or origin,” he wrote, emphasizing a tenet of France’s republican universalism. Many of the French players reiterated Araud’s sentiment. “The France of today is a France full of different colors,” the midfielder Paul Pogba said. “We all feel French. We are all happy to wear this jersey.” The defender Benjamin Mendy replied to a post that had added foreign-flag emojis to various players’ names, changing them back to the Tricolor. “Fixed 😉,” he wrote. A broad consensus emerged after the 2018 victory and during the early days of President Emmanuel Macron’s first term that discouraged racial division and identity politics—what the French call communautarisme. It sidelined the far right’s complaints of “replacement” and emphasized an accommodating, assimilating color-blindness. Today, France has become the world’s greatest incubator of soccer talent. The Paris region accounted for more players in the World Cup than any other metropolitan area on Earth. The country has become so dominant that it produces too many players for Les Bleus: Of the 1,248 athletes in this year’s tournament, 99 were born in France. In acknowledgment of their popularity, Jordan Bardella, the 30-year-old head of the RN, praised the team as it advanced through the competition—and held his tongue when it was eliminated. Thomas Chatterton Williams: The homicide upending French politics Mbappé, the captain of Les Bleus, has begun to channel the team’s growing cultural capital into politics. Speaking with Vanity Fair earlier this year, he expressed concern about a potential National Rally victory in 2027. “People sometimes think that because you have money, because you’re famous, these kinds of problems don’t affect you,” he said, referring to the social divisions that the party’s rhetoric foments. “But it affects me, because I know what it means, and what kind of consequences it can have for my country when ​those kinds of people take control.” Wembanyama, for his part, has been more reserved. In 2024, shortly before parliamentary elections in which RN won its largest ever number of seats, he spoke diplomatically: “Political choices are personal. But I think that, at least for me, it’s important to move away from extremes.” Still, no matter how vocal they are or how desirable they make diversity seem, athletes or other exceptional individuals can never legitimize a multicultural society on their own. As Americans have learned in the post-Obama era, symbols of integration are necessary but insufficient. In fact, they can even help racists and xenophobes conceal regression. Many people on the far right, for example, point to the popularity of an Obama or an Mbappé as evidence that their society is already tolerant enough. For all that the French right gets wrong, some critics of rapid demographic change identify serious problems, even if they go on to draw grotesque conclusions. A 10-minute walk from the plaza where I watched Les Bleus win in 2018, a quasi-permanent tent city is populated by migrants from Africa and the Middle East, one of the many lingering reminders that France has failed to address a refugee crisis that has been exacerbated by increased migration. Cultural representation cannot solve this problem. France needs leaders who can improve the material outcomes of all citizens, irrespective of racial, religious, or ethnic lines. Moreover, politicians must safeguard France’s common life, which means addressing the instability that migration can cause, rather than forfeiting that task to the ascendant far right. Only then can representation lead to mutual recognition, obligation, and belonging. Any society should applaud diversity in moments of triumph. But that applause won’t mean much if its leadership can’t manage demographic change in the intervals between.

One possible way out would be to specify in federal law that data that can enable mass surveillance must be protected - leaking it gets you fined, government doesn't get it without a warrant. The HIPAA laws are a potential model here - they basically say data related to health care can only be shared between certain businesses, and only with the permission of the person the data is about. There may be more comments in this discussion. Without JavaScript enabled, you might want to turn on Classic Discussion System in your preferences instead. The nicest thing about the Alto is that it doesn't run faster at night.

Nobody in American Politics Can Figure Out When to Back Down
2026-08-08

Nobody in American Politics Can Figure Out When to Back Down

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Surge, the newsletter that covers the most important political nonsense of the week, delivered to your inbox every Saturday. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that encourages you to get some rest this August. It’s going to be a long fall campaign season talking about the only issue that matters in America: Hasan Piker. Today we review some results from a big week of primaries and explore what could have possibly compelled Jeanine Pirro to tell the truth in a legal filing. Todd Blanche’s attorney general nomination came down to one famous Senate pushover. Is it possible he could get pushed over? Let’s begin, though, with a look toward next week’s most-watched primary, and Fox News’ next big celebrity-in-waiting. The national spotlight is now fully thrust on Francesca Hong, the democratic socialist and front-runner for Tuesday’s Wisconsin Democratic gubernatorial primary. The topic of the week was Thanksgiving, after a 2020 tweet of Hong’s calling to “cancel Thanksgiving” resurfaced. In a CNN interview this week, then, she was asked whether she still believed that the holiday should be canceled. She did not give a firm answer. Another day or so later, she told Politico that Thanksgiving is her favorite holiday, that “I don’t want to cancel it,” and that “I actually love it.” Hong rose in the polls by consolidating left-wing primary voters behind a populist economic platform of expanding social services, taxing the rich, and crushing data centers—all against a splintered opposition. But now that she’s at the top, everything she has ever said or posted will be under national examination. We don’t have any advice for Hong on how she should answer these sorts of questions about past tweets sent from deep inside the bubble. We would point out, though, that governor’s races are not like Senate or presidential elections, in which pure partisanship ensures that each candidate in a swing state like Wisconsin starts at 47 percent. General election voters are more flexible in state-level races, which don’t have any effect on whether Donald Trump can continue getting judges confirmed. And we’re hard-pressed to think of anyone who’s won a governor’s race after struggling to answer a question about whether Thanksgiving should be canceled. Some great news: This will be our last entry about the Democratic Senate primary in Michigan, because guess what? It actually happened this week, after what felt like 47 years of campaigning. As the polls predicted, progressive Abdul El-Sayed defeated moderate Rep. Haley Stevens. But as the polls did not predict, it was a squeaker: El-Sayed won by only a point. Our first reaction was that the lack of a lopsided verdict meant that El-Sayed would have to work that much harder to bring the party together. But then we saw that as a potential positive. El-Sayed admits he can be cocky, and the result called for an immediate conciliatory tone to welcome skeptical Stevens voters. Among the principals, everyone—El-Sayed, Stevens, Chuck Schumer, the Democratic Senatorial Campaign Committee—quickly got on the same team. Republicans, lining up behind former Rep. Mike Rogers, immediately revealed that their campaign strategy would rely on scaring voters out of electing a Muslim. Shocker. Do you think we’ll be able to skip this race in next week’s edition? If only we could promise such luxuries … By the time you read this, it’s entirely possible that the Senate will have voted to confirm Todd Blanche as attorney general. Because, by midday Friday, Trump’s pick appeared to have the votes. Early in the week, the two senators who were holding up Blanche in committee—Thom Tillis and John Cornyn—signed off on his nomination after Blanche formally rescinded the order establishing Trump’s $1.8 billion “anti-weaponization” fund. Once out of committee, the administration had to secure the support of either Bill Cassidy, Lisa Murkowski, or Susan Collins. Collins quickly said she wouldn’t support him. (We should’ve known, then and there, that Blanche would be fine.) Then, early Friday morning, Murkowski said she wouldn’t either. So Cassidy had a free shot on an open goal. With one vote, he could stick it to the president who had cost him his reelection bid. He could salvage his reputation following his infamous vote for Robert F. Kennedy Jr. He could, in short, live a little bit. Cassidy announced, in a Senate floor speech late Friday morning, that he would support Blanche after all. It came down to something we mentioned a few weeks ago: fear of the alternative. “The choice is not between perfection and Mr. Blanche,” Cassidy said. “It is between Mr. Blanche and another acting attorney general who may not run the department effectively under President Trump, and who indeed may not be as good as Mr. Blanche.” A normal administration might be grateful for Cassidy’s support, under the circumstances—he’s going to get an earful for flinching, once again—and proceed cautiously. But we’d soon expect this Justice Department to find a way to rub it in his eye as a parting gift. One House Republican—North Carolina Rep. Chuck Edwards—dropped his reelection bid this week under a growing cloud of scandal. Here’s the problem for House Republican leaders: They needed two members to pack it up, and Ohio Rep. Max Miller has not budged. Details of his messy divorce and child custody battle, which include allegations (which Miller denies) of mental and physical abuse, have reached a breaking point for the party. Miller’s former father-in-law, Sen. Bernie Moreno, broke his long silence over the matter. “As he has admitted privately,” Moreno posted on X, “Max Miller needs serious psychological help. He is a danger to my daughter, and I hold my breath every minute he has custody of my granddaughter.” He called on him to quit and injected further tales of alleged cruelty into the discussion. Numerous other colleagues of Moreno’s have as well. Miller has blown through one deadline to withdraw from reelection, missing the Wednesday cutoff that would trigger a special primary election to replace him on the November ballot. If he withdraws by Monday, though, local Republican officials in his district could approve a new nominee. Miller has shown no indication that he’s willing to do that. As he explained in a deeply uncomfortable CNN interview this week, “My daughter is going to grow up one day, and she’s going to see all of this, and she’s not going to see that her father caved to political and social media pressures for something that he never did.” But that Monday deadline—and more pressure from Trump, who has waffled about this publicly but would not like to lose the House—could focus his otherwise scattered mind. U.S. Attorney for the District of Columbia Jeanine Pirro crossed a bright red line late last Friday when she did the unthinkable: She told the truth in court (!) about what had happened with the Reflecting Pool renovation. In seeking to dismiss a felony charge against David Hearn, the former Olympian charged with ripping off a piece of new pool liner, Pirro explained in a filing that the pool’s “damage was the result of a botched installation and not vandalism.” This shocked us at first, as Pirro would have known better than anyone ahead of time that Trump would take this poorly. And he sure did, fuming at her publicly and privately for several days in a row. But Pirro had a plan to keep her job. She loaded boxes of evidence into a White House meeting with Trump and Interior Secretary Doug Burgum to prove to the president that it was Burgum who had caused this problem for the president. The plan worked well enough that Pirro—for now, at least—still has a job. Apologizing to Trump will not work. It’s weak, after all. Anger redirection, though, has some potential. One of the more bewildering primary results of the year came this week in Michigan’s 8th Congressional District, a swing seat currently represented by Democratic freshman Rep. Kristen McDonald Rivet. Amir Hassan, the Navy veteran backed by Trump and GOP congressional leaders, was flattened in his primary by a certain Thomas Smith, a rando who had suspended his campaign (and endorsed a third candidate, Al Lemmo). To give you a sense of the credibility of Smith’s candidacy, his only campaign finance filing to the Federal Election Commission was a handwritten note stating that he had raised $1,850 and that he couldn’t file it electronically “because I have had no success logging in and have not been able to get a response from the helpdesk.” Our first reaction to this story was that it was extremely funny. After further thought, though, it nets out to merely above-average funny. There’s only one reason a credible, Trump-backed candidate named Amir Hassan would have lost this Republican primary, and it’s not pretty. Tennessee held its primaries on Thursday, because Thursday primaries are just how Tennessee rolls. The main news of the evening was that Tennessee Rep. Andy Ogles, a Freedom Caucus member and shit-stirrer, lost his primary to former state Agriculture Commissioner Charlie Hatcher. Ogles had Trump’s endorsement; Hatcher had the endorsement of Tennessee Gov. Bill Lee. Alongside a history of campaign finance investigations, Ogles can be a jerk. He tweeted on separate occasions this year that “Muslims don’t belong in American society” and that “homosexuality has no place in America.” (The latter he deleted and blamed on a staffer.) So why, in spite of those remarks, did he lose a Republican primary in Tennessee? One factor was that Hatcher outraised and outspent Ogles. But redistricting played a big role too. The state redistricted this year after the Supreme Court nuked another section of the Voting Rights Act, eliminating the Memphis-based district held by Democrats. That meant that Ogles was running in a new, hideously gerrymandered district snaking from just outside Nashville all the way to Memphis. The Associated Press estimated that 82 percent of the 5th Congressional District’s voters were new to it. And these voters decided that Andy Ogles would have no place in, and would not belong to, the next Congress. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Senate Floor Speeches Rarely Matter. This One Did. And It Was Terrible.
2026-08-07

Senate Floor Speeches Rarely Matter. This One Did. And It Was Terrible.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-07

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. On Friday, Bill Cassidy of Louisiana took to the Senate floor to announce his intent to vote for Todd Blanche, Donald Trump’s nominee to be attorney general. Cassidy’s support almost certainly means that Blanche, who faced tough questioning from even some of Cassidy’s fellow Republicans over his loyalty to the president, will be confirmed as the country’s chief law-enforcement officer. Cassidy, who lost his reelection bid this year after voting in 2021 to convict Trump over Jan. 6, seemed hyperaware of his swing-vote status. For six minutes and 30 seconds, he laid out his case for confirming Blanche. But it turned out to be a weak case, one that falls apart under the weight of its own contradictions, omissions, and leaps of logic. Today’s newsletter offers a close reading. Cassidy opened with an attempt to reframe the debate. “Mr. Blanche has become a referendum on how people feel about President Trump,” he said. “I have not approached it that way.” Instead, Cassidy continued, the vote should hinge on how Blanche “will go forward in a difficult job.” But there’s a reason people see Blanche’s nomination that way, even if Cassidy prefers not to. Before elevating Blanche, Trump fired the previous attorney general, Pam Bondi, in part because she failed to prosecute his political opponents fast enough. Trump seems to view Blanche as someone he can more easily control, which makes Cassidy’s choice to confirm him inextricable from the president’s efforts to bend the department to his will. Cassidy next noted that Blanche, who has led the Department of Justice in an acting capacity since April, could continue to do so even if senators reject him. Then he argued that a Senate stamp of approval will give Blanche “greater leverage to push for things that should be pushed for” in the form of buy-in from rank-and-file department staff—and “more leverage to push back” on the president. Whether Blanche will meaningfully stand up to Trump remains to be seen; despite trying to distance himself during his confirmation hearing, Blanche at one point accidentally called himself the president’s lawyer. But setting that aside, it’s mighty strange to argue that putting a check on Trump requires confirming his handpicked nominee. By Cassidy’s own logic, wouldn’t Senate confirmation also give Blanche more “leverage” to execute Trump’s pet projects, not just ones that are in the country’s interest? From there, Cassidy moved to mollify his own fears. Cassidy first criticized Blanche for approving a proposed $1.8 billion “anti-weaponization fund” to funnel taxpayer money to Trump allies (including, potentially, pardoned Jan. 6 rioters). Then he noted that Blanche had “made commitments” to shut down the fund in exchange for the votes of two other GOP senators. (At this point Cassidy couldn’t resist a bit of whataboutism, accusing “presidents of both parties” of siccing the law on their foes.) But then Cassidy did something strange. “I’m not entirely satisfied where this is,” he said of the fund, “and I have no doubts that I’ll have concern in the future.” So why confirm Blanche if his commitments aren’t satisfactory? Cassidy has been down this road before. Last year, he supplied a key vote to confirm Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as Trump’s Health and Human Services secretary. Cassidy, a doctor who criticized Kennedy’s anti-vaccine conspiracy theories, ultimately backed him after Kennedy promised not to fire members of a vaccine advisory group or muck with language on the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s website affirming that vaccines don’t cause autism. When Kennedy ended up violating those pledges, Cassidy disagreed with him from the sidelines but could do little else. Cassidy is clearly no fan of Trump’s attempts to use the DOJ for retribution. “A never-ending cycle of prosecuting political enemies undermines the rule of law, degrades the public’s trust in our institutions, and makes our politics more extreme when we need to unite to solve the big problems of our time,” Cassidy intoned, before praising Blanche for, he said, focusing the department on violent crime, human and drug trafficking, and fraud. But Blanche has been a participant, not just a spectator, in Trump’s efforts to weaponize justice. He has overseen transparently pretextual accusations the DOJ has leveled against Trump foes, including former FBI Director James Comey, alleged Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool vandals, and others—none of which Cassidy mentioned. Seems like a big omission! Throughout, Cassidy cast himself as a realist. “The choice,” he claimed, “is not between perfection and Mr. Blanche. It is between Mr. Blanche and another acting attorney general who may not run the department effectively under President Trump and who, indeed, may not be as good as Mr. Blanche.” This is a wild misreading of the situation. For one thing, Blanche may not be as good as Cassidy thinks (see above). But even if he is, Blanche is up for the job only because Trump chose to fire Bondi, whom the Senate also confirmed. So if he does push back, why wouldn’t Trump just run the same playbook and replace Blanche with an even more servile minion? Cassidy closed with what sounded like a lament. “Ultimately,” he said, “decisions are made by the president.” Trump may be “unrelenting” in his pursuit of political prosecutions, Cassidy added, “but this will be true no matter who the attorney general is.” This is a depressingly resigned and remarkably fatalistic admission from a U.S. senator. If Cassidy is right that no one can slake Trump’s thirst for political vengeance, what’s the point of confirming Blanche at all? And if he’s really so convinced of Trump’s motivations, he has options other than rubber-stamping the president’s nominees. Cassidy might, for example, refuse to confirm Blanche unless Trump signs legislation restricting how long a government official can serve in an acting capacity or adopts roadblocks to political prosecutions. The fact that he isn’t is a tell about even Trump-critical Republicans’ appetite to defy the president. If a senator Trump effectively ran out of office won’t even stand up to him, why would Blanche? Which leads to one final question: Why did Cassidy really back Blanche? Maybe he’s convinced himself of a flimsy case. Maybe Senate leadership leaned on him or he’s covering for a colleague who faces a tough reelection. Or maybe he just doesn’t care that much. Asked last year if he regretted voting to confirm Kennedy, Cassidy demurred. “You live life forward,” he said. “Let the day’s own troubles be sufficient for the day.” If Blanche does end up playing Cassidy for a fool, at least we know that the senator won’t spend too much time looking back. Congrats! After another long stretch of unrelenting news, the weekend is finally nigh. To help you enjoy it, my colleagues suggest: Something to look forward to on Monday: Picture a time when plans didn’t require a 12-person group chat, three calendar apps, and a poll just to grab a drink. When you could just … show up? In honor of Slate’s 30th birthday, Dan Kois is challenging you, and your crew, to mentally travel (via newsletter) back to that time and rediscover the lost art of hanging out. Sign up to do the challenges along with him here! (And please enjoy this delightful video of Dan and Slate’s Kate Lindsay having a definitely-not-staged phone call to discuss the challenge!) Test your news knowledge: Between Iranian cyberattacks, wildfires, and the Michigan Democratic Senate primary, a lot has happened this week. How closely have you been following the headlines? See if you can get more answers right on the news quiz than Slate’s senior director of podcast operations. One movie to skip, and one not to miss: If you’re having trouble deciding what to watch this weekend, Sam Adams is here to help. First, what to skip: Sam writes that The Last House, Greta Lee’s new flick about a family of four trapped inside their suburban home, offers a pretty self-serving solution for how to spend the end of the world. Instead, consider Jane Schoenbrun’s Teenage Sex and Death at Camp Miasma, which riffs on slasher films like Sleepaway Camp and Friday the 13th. Sam writes that it’s a brilliant, funny, and blood-soaked manifesto on how to love people who have less-than-perfect politics. A very revealing investigation: When did everyone get so shy about nudity? Two generations ago, it wasn’t that weird to be naked in front of other people. Now everyone hates it. To find out what changed, Rowan Jacobsen stripped down and visited the last nudist resort in Southern California. His rollicking investigation might make you yearn for the liberation of a tranquil day spent in the buff. And with that, enjoy the rest of your Friday evening! We’ll see you next week. The Real Reason Rand Paul Put a DOJ Target on Anthony Fauci’s Back
2026-08-07

The Real Reason Rand Paul Put a DOJ Target on Anthony Fauci’s Back

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-08-07

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story each week about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back. You’ll also receive updates on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Dr. Anthony Fauci is a name most Americans probably didn’t think they would have to hear about again, but Senate Republicans have different plans in mind. This week, the Senate Homeland Security Committee voted to hold Fauci in contempt of court for not answering questions during a hearing meant to force his sworn testimony on the United States’ response to the COVID-19 pandemic. The 85-year-old scientist invoked his right to plead the Fifth Amendment more than 100 times and Republicans eviscerated him for this, arguing that because former President Joe Biden preemptively pardoned Fauci, he no longer could claim Fifth Amendment protection. This is simply false, and in fact, Republicans themselves are continuing to give Fauci legal cover to stay silent as they pursue a farcical investigation against yet another perceived political enemy. Kentucky Sen. Rand Paul, chair of the Homeland Security Committee, has long despised Fauci, going so far as to suggest the scientist should be thrown in prison. Paul alleges Fauci lied to Congress about the origins of the deadly virus and that he unnecessarily pressured states to close schools down during the pandemic, mask up, and mandate vaccination. Fauci has denied all of this, but with an emboldened GOP consuming Washington, Paul has found his time to strike. Days before Fauci was scheduled to testify, Paul released more than 1,000 pages of Fauci’s personal diary, in which the scientist chronicled behind-the-scenes meetings and discussions with Trump White House officials, fellow scientists, and doctors alongside his own assumptions and theories about how COVID-19 came to be. And this week, Republican Sen. Ron Johnson, who also sits on the Homeland Security Committee, announced he had gained possession of Fauci’s government-issued cellphone, courtesy of the Department of Health and Human Services. The Biden administration foresaw this political attack and preemptively pardoned Fauci, along with lawmakers who served on the House Select Committee on Jan. 6 and members of the Biden family. For Fauci, this pardon protects all of his actions taken between 2014 through January 19, 2025, from federal prosecution only. On its face, this would make it seem as though Fauci is perfectly safe from Paul and other potentially vindictive Republicans still salty about mask and vaccine mandates from five years ago. That is not so. “When [Fauci] went into that hearing, he had at least two reasons to be concerned that he could have criminal liability resulting from what he says,” Frank Bowman, professor emeritus of law at the University of Missouri and a former federal and state prosecutor, told me. “First, the possibility that he might say something false and be prosecutable for perjury under federal law. Second, possibility that something he said might conceivably be used by some state government to prosecute him criminally.” Bowman explained that the nature of Fauci’s job during the pandemic, which was over the course of about three years, required him to make many public statements on a variety of different platforms, from congressional hearings to TV hits, scientific conferences, consumer-facing events, and classrooms. This makes it pretty easy for a maliciously disposed senator or prosecutor to find some inconsistency between what Fauci may have said the other day versus something he said five years ago. “The other thing is that Fauci’s views over time, like those of any good scientist, changed in varying degrees on varying topics,” Bowman said. This is particularly true when it comes to Fauci’s diary, where Paul and other Republicans can easily cherry-pick certain entries and find a way to demonstrate inconsistencies from a “harried government official trying to respond to a global calamity,” Bowman said. “What he says throughout that record, taken sequentially, is not always going to be entirely consistent. And sometimes there are going to be changes of view.” Another critical point that likely also supported Fauci’s decision to plead the Fifth is the fact that it is simply naive at this point to assume the Justice Department is operating with even an ounce of integrity. “In olden times—that is, before two years ago—I think one in Dr. Fauci’s position could have confidently relied on the Justice Department not to take a referral from an obviously vindictive senator and try to prosecute him,” Bowman said. Today, as Todd Blanche prepares to take the top job at the DOJ, the acting attorney general has already proven absolute fealty to President Donald Trump. He’s spent the past 20 months bringing numerous vindictive prosecutions that Trump has demanded, all while committing disturbing abuses of federal law and destroying any semblance of a presumption of regularity. This alone plausibly gives Fauci a reason to abstain from saying a word to Republicans, let alone under oath. There’s also the fact that at least four states, Florida, Louisiana, Alabama, and West Virginia, have vowed to initiate state investigations against Fauci, where he does not enjoy pardon protection. Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier already issued an investigative subpoena to Fauci this week. When you consider all of this together, it’s not surprising that Fauci invoked his Fifth Amendment right. Nevertheless, Paul and his fellow Republicans on the Homeland Security Committee voted to hold Fauci in contempt, and now the DOJ gets to decide what to do with him. Bowman doesn’t believe it’s a given that Fauci will be prosecuted, considering Trump has not really said much about him. Plus, Paul is not one of the president’s favorite lackeys, given he’s repeatedly voted to pull U.S. troops out of the Iran war, and said that “by any objective measure we’re worse off than we were before the war.” However, Fauci’s contempt of Congress is not too dissimilar to when former Attorney General Merrick Garland indicted Steve Bannon and Peter Navarro for failing to show up on Capitol Hill for sworn testimony related to the Jan. 6 insurrection. Both men ended up serving prison time. Plus, in a few short months there’s a strong possibility that the levers of power in Congress will be handed to Democrats, many of whom are frothing at the mouth to investigate Trump and basically every member of his administration; is Fauci the hill Blanche’s DOJ really wants to die on right now? Say the DOJ does decide to prosecute Fauci for contempt of Congress; Bowman believes that it likely won’t hold up in court anyway. First, Paul bypassed the full Senate and simply held a committee vote, which is technically a violation of internal Senate rules and federal statute. Second, the merits of this case do not bode well for the DOJ, as a judge would likely find Fauci had a constitutional right to plead the Fifth, given Paul’s past comments about him and the ongoing state investigations. What it seems to be coming down to is Republicans attempting to replicate Trump’s playbook: targeting a longtime foe simply out of spite, knowing deep down it will amount to next to nothing but will possibly succeed in embarrassing Fauci and costing him a pretty penny in legal fees. Ultimately, like the false commentary about Fauci itself, any attempt to force a prosecution may be much ado about nothing. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. London still dominates Britain's datacenter map, but the regions are powering up
2026-08-10

London still dominates Britain's datacenter map, but the regions are powering up

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-10

Visit Original Website
The capital holds two-thirds of capacity, while multimegawatt projects gather beyond the M25 A snapshot of Britain's datacenter estate puts nearly 40 percent of its facilities in London, along with about two-thirds of its estimated colocation capacity, although development is spreading beyond the capital. The analysis counts 555 UK server farms, matching Statista's latest figure. It puts their combined IT capacity at about 1.6 GW. By facility count, that would put Blighty second only to the US, although there is a considerable gap between the two. The study data was compiled by Karas Plating, a UK-based specialist in electroplating and surface coatings for critical electrical components and power distribution systems. The firm presumably has an eye on where new business might emerge as new datacenters are planned as part of the government's AI Opportunities Action Plan. It will surprise nobody that London is the nexus of the UK's data infrastructure, with clusters of bit barns to serve the financial markets in the City, and along the M4 motorway corridor close to the fiber-optic cable routes that stretch west from the capital to join the undersea cables linking with North America. London has 219 active facilities, according to the analysis, or about 40 percent of the total. This adds up to 1,048 MW, or two-thirds of the country's entire datacenter compute capacity. It also leads the way in future growth with 15 planned expansion projects. But the report also indicates there is a significant shift taking place toward high-capacity regional infrastructure to support growing demand. This is something The Register has previously reported on, as developers look away from London due to issues like power shortages and planning constraints. Outside the Big Smoke, South East England claims 62 current facilities, adding up to 128 MW capacity, with ten planned developments, the second-biggest expansion queue nationwide. We assume that these include bit barns close to London's M25 orbital motorway, such as the massive campus planned by colocation firm Equinix at South Mimms. Further afield, North West England has 39 active facilities with 52 MW capacity, and five upcoming builds. This makes it the region with the largest number of planned projects outside of the traditional hot spot of London and the South East. The East of England and Scotland both have more operational datacenters, the first with 45 and a total of 44 MW capacity, while north of the border there are no fewer than 49 with a total of 30 MW. Wales currently boasts 32 active sites, but these make for a total of 154 MW of IT capacity, coming in second only to London. The region with the fewest current operational server farms is the South West, which has 20 sites adding up to 54 MW, and two new facilities planned. One of these is presumably the controversial Devon data campus near Great Torrington, which is claimed will house 1.5 GW of AI-optimized compute capacity. Just ahead is North East England, where there are 21 sites with a total of 17 MW capacity. This will change in the near future, however, as US investment giant Blackstone has plans for a huge facility near Blyth, Northumberland, said to be large enough for 720 MW of IT. OpenAI also aims to build a Stargate datacenter project at Cobalt Park, North Tyneside, although it has put these plans on hold for now, citing reasons such as the cost of energy in the UK. ® Repairable hardware is little comfort when personal details escape PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. Now it has to grow up fast and evolve for the AI era. Smart glasses are only smart if we train them to be good. Then they'll be fantastic
2026-08-10

Smart glasses are only smart if we train them to be good. Then they'll be fantastic

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-10

Visit Original Website
Just add smart humans – and a pinch of dog The most sobering observation in tech is not how much AI is taking on the appearance of the human mind. It's how much the human mind increasingly resembles Windows – not in functionality, although we all know that person, but in the way it crufts up and loses compatibility after years without an upgrade or reboot. We start shiny and new, with everything around us equally exciting, yet end up cursing all these damn changes and godawful broccoli-haired vibe coders. AI has turned many of the finest minds of their generation into Luddites, fearful for their jobs, status, and dignity. Augmented reality pushes people back even further. With its ability to record, recognize, and categorize all that it sees, it is the silent, invisible spirit in a demonic pact with its users. We no longer fear for our jobs; we fear for our souls. Hence the reflexive bans on Meta-style sin-oculars, everywhere from cafes in Seattle to DEF CON, where tech provocations to the status quo are normally welcome. The reasons for such bans are obvious, evidence-backed, and seemingly inarguable. Security, privacy, and dignity are all poisoned and traduced by always-on 21st century audio, video, and who knows what other sensors disguised as everyday human objects with an 800-year history. Ban the buggers. Nobody would argue with that. There are, as always, counterarguments, although they lack the emotional force of an instinctive recoil. The logic is there nonetheless. Banning a technology rarely achieves its aims without collateral damage. Clandestine wireless bugs are frowned upon everywhere, obtainable everywhere, and used everywhere. Radio itself exists on a continuum of utility, from the dark side of bugging and jamming and spoofing and soft rock broadcasters, to the lovely light of Wi-Fi and mobile and the whispers of creation caught in giant telescopes. Augmented reality has its own spectrum, from the ghastly to the essential, even when you peel back the techbro hype. Very little of the good stuff requires covert live video recording. Put simply, people with GoPros on gimbals, strapped to their hats, or worn as conspicuous bodycams behave much better than people with spycams while retaining most of the useful applications. For others, smart glasses have the potential – if not yet quite the reality – of being life-changing. If you can't see well, but your AI companion can, and if the world isn't built to be visually accessible to your personal peculiarities, but your AI companion is… well, making the blind see has a decent track record of divine approval. DEF CON is great if you can see the screens and navigate the crowds. It's rubbish otherwise. Here is technology that could give all that back to you, only to be told you cannot have it. Imagine smart glasses as dogs. If by dog you mean aggressive wolf-derived predator, the preferred weapon of drug lords, with a track record of mauling babies and defecating in your dahlias, then it is a terrible thing. If by dog you mean an intelligent helper, loving companion, and loyal protector capable of guiding its human safely through danger, it is an indispensable, life-enhancing thing that should not be denied to those who need it. Both are true, and society has developed workable ways of accommodating one while controlling the other. Nobody would ever allow a Doberman in an operating theater (if you have counter-examples, please keep them to yourself). Likewise, few complain at "no dogs except guide dogs" policies at venues. We know their behavior, we know what they look like with white harnesses and reflective tabards. That's where we can and should go with smart glasses. Agree on acceptable behavior and a way to signal it to everyone within eyeshot. Allow accommodations where necessary. Develop the wherewithal – wear-with-all? – to find and control transgressors, creeps, and predators. The protocols will vary: few complain when asked to surrender electronic devices before entering a secure area, but a pub trying the same thing would get short shrift. It has the same shape as the path humans and dogs have followed since wolves first circled our campfires. Humans ran that show, and the outcome beat hunting the creatures to extinction. Domestication took many thousands of years, while we probably have five at most to work things out with smart glasses. On the plus side, even the worst-engineered pair cannot rip out our throats while we sleep (see above with what to do with counter-examples, thanks). It's best not to wait for unguided evolution to take its course. There is the possibility that smart glasses will follow the example of smartphones and sprout enough lenses that they grow to resemble the compound eyes of a mutant insect alien brain-hugger, in which case the problems will solve themselves. But while the industry remains determined to disguise its technology as ordinary fashionable eyewear, manufacturers must be pressed to show and tell. No, Meta, a small red light that can't be masked, honest, is not going to save you. Color-coding the frames to disclose capabilities, compulsory QR codes that ID what the glasses are and who's wearing them? Now you're talking. It'll kill the market? Find another way to tame your dogs, then. But they do need to be trained. Doubtless there were many around those campfires who had lived long enough to fully fear the encroachment of wolves. A long investment in the way things are makes change expensive, especially when the dangers are real and the benefits still theoretical. Yet change happened, and the descendants of those wolves helped the descendants of those humans to gain virtual sight. The rest of us did quite well out of it too. "No" is not the answer. The answer is "Yes, if." Good dog. ® Repairable hardware is little comfort when personal details escape PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. Now it has to grow up fast and evolve for the AI era. Demoralized developer's desperate hack came back to haunt him on LinkedIn
2026-08-10

Demoralized developer's desperate hack came back to haunt him on LinkedIn

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-10

Visit Original Website
A script that shouldn't have worked, on a project that never ended, for a company that hardly cared WHO, ME? The past may be a foreign country, but it's one The Register likes to explore each Friday in "Who, Me?" – our reader-contributed column that shares your tales of workplace mistakes and great escapes. This week, meet a reader we'll Regomize as "Walter," who once worked for one half of a duopoly competing in the intertwined markets for bad poetry celebrating memorable life moments and questionable television dramatizing the sentiments in those poems. The duopoly didn't make for a stable industry. "We produced a steady cycle of layoffs and hiring," Walter lamented. He nonetheless survived the latest round of layoffs and found himself upgrading systems from Python 2 to Python 3. "Our vendors had stopped pretending Python 2 was still a reasonable life choice," he told The Register. That move coincided with an upgrade to a new version of Linux that happily ran Python 3. "The older one technically could too, but only after a series of rituals involving symbolic links, configuration tweaks, and what I can only describe as informed superstition. It worked on about 80 percent of our systems and the remaining 20 percent flatly refused to cooperate for reasons nobody could explain." Walter told us he blamed the problem on a recent change of vendor in the office cafeteria. "It fit the evidence about as well as anything else," he wrote. Everyone on the team knew the upgrade-resistant 20 percent had to be fixed, but the recent redundancies had left morale so low that nobody was willing to pull the necessary all-nighters. The fact that some of the staff had decided to drink their way through the situation didn't help either. "One morning, fueled by spectacularly bad coffee and the news that a job interview had been pushed back another week, I had what can only be described as a terrible idea," Walter admitted. "What if I simply pointed Python 3 at Python 2?" He tried it by adding the following symbolic link to a script: ln -s /usr/bin/python2 /usr/bin/python3 "I expected fire. Or alarms. Or at least one very concerned email," he told The Register. "Instead... it worked. Not 'mostly worked.' It worked well enough that nothing complained, nothing paged us, and nobody noticed." Walter celebrated his success by leaving the following comment in the configuration script: "Yeah, yeah, yeah. I know, I know. At least I was mostly sober so it can't be THAT dumb!" Eight years later, Walter had thankfully moved on to another job, although he still checked LinkedIn occasionally. During one visit to Microsoft's bottomless trove of B2B sales advice, he spotted a message from one of the remaining workers at the poetry/television company. The survivor reported that the last Linux upgrade was finally complete. "They'd also finally found the symbolic link, after it survived a move from a script to Ansible," Walter told Who, Me? Has a moment of exasperation or desperation come back to haunt you? If so, don't get frustrated: click here to share your story with Who, Me? Our mailbag has seen better days, so fresh contributions are especially welcome. ® Repairable hardware is little comfort when personal details escape PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. Linus Torvalds says AI has made 'huge' Linux kernel updates the new normal
2026-08-10

Linus Torvalds says AI has made 'huge' Linux kernel updates the new normal

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-08-10

Visit Original Website
He’s not thrilled, but won’t let it delay the release of version 7.2 Linux kernel boss Linus Torvalds thinks the project has reached a new normal of supersized updates, thanks to AI. Torvalds posts a weekly state of the kernel update, and last week’s message opened with his observation that “This rc is huge.” “Even by the ‘new normal’ standards this is a big rc, and I think it's the biggest rc6 we've had in years at least by commit count,” he wrote on August 2. Fast forward a week and the size of the release candidate remains a concern. “I can't say that I'm exactly thrilled about the size of this all,” Torvalds wrote in the message that announces the seventh release candidate for Linux 7.2. “But it is what it is: the new normal with a lot of fixes, many of them due to review by various AI tools.” Torvalds has a complex relationship with AI. He’s previously railed against the idea that good documentation can explain the work of developers who use AI to contribute poor code to the kernel, and also complained that the Linux kernel security mailing list has become almost unmanageable due to a flood of AI-generated bug reports, many of them duplicates. Torvalds has also declared “Linux is not one of those anti-AI projects,” and therefore all-but-welcomed machine-made contributions to the project. While Torvalds is concerned about the size of rc7, he said “nothing looks particularly scary per se - it's just that there's a lot here.” “Most of it is fairly small,” he wrote, before calling out three bigger changes: s390/zcrypt fixes, the return of the btrfs fixup worker infrastructure, and some netfilter ipset fixes. “But aside from a few places like that, most of this is just lots of tiny fixes. It's pretty much spread all over - drivers (gpu, sound, networking, you name it), filesystems, core networking, arch code.” All those little changes don’t add up to a reason to delay the release of Linux 7.2, and Torvalds wrote “I would expect that to happen next weekend unless something really bad pops up.” By next Sunday, the world should therefore have a new version of the Linux kernel. Version 7.2 won’t be a long-term support release, but will include significant features such as improved scheduling for GPUs and CPUs. ® Repairable hardware is little comfort when personal details escape PARTNER CONTENT: Platform engineering won the argument. PLUS: Hiveminds are emerging to hack the planet, and open-weight models are the new new red scare. KETTLE OpenAI's invasion of Hugging Face keeps getting worse somehow, Chinese open-weight models are nigh on to reaching parity with their closed-off American cousins,  and AI crawlers are getting their own LLM-poisoning ads. Were there anything world-shaking events in AI land we missed this week? You can listen to the latest episode of The Kettle right here on this page, as well as on Spotify, Apple Music, or YouTube, where you can subscribe to get notified about the latest episode.Join Kettle host Brandon Vigliarolo as he chats with systems editor Tobias Mann and senior reporter Tom Claburn about this trio of exciting AI stories from the week. Worried that a rogue hivemind of AI agents could come for your secrets? Want reassurance that a Chinese open-weight takeover wouldn't be that bad? Curious how LLMs are being advertised to when you're not watching? That's all on tap for the latest episode. Hello everyone and welcome to another episode of The Register's Kettle podcast. I'm Reg reporter Brandon Vigliarolo, and this week we've got a few AI stories to round up on everything from the latest in the OpenAI Hugging Face fiasco to news that AI crawlers are now being served their own model altering ads. With me to discuss this and more this week is our systems editor Tobias Mann and senior reporter Tom Claiburn. Thanks for coming on, guys. So let's start with the latest in the OpenAI agentic hacking of Hugging Face. We got some updates this week from Black Hat. And it seems since that's come out, every frontier lab under the sun is racing to tell us how dangerous their models are and that they can do it too. But the original of these incidents is still we're still learning more about it and it's still getting scarier. our cybersecurity editor Jess Lyons was in Vegas this week and she's a little tied up still. So, Tom, can you explain to us what we learned from Black Hat this week? The discussion was from a session that was done by two OpenAI researchers and it didn't really provide a ton of view information but it did fill in a lot of the gaps about the timelines and how this happened and they're promising a full postmortem at some point. But one of the things that came out of it was the how long this had been going on and some of the tactics that these models used to essentially gain internet access when they didn't have it. And these things were mentioned in the various posts by OpenAI and by Hugging Face. But the talk provided a little bit more detail about how it happened, and it's really interesting because they mentioned, I think in the initial post, about the AI models used a message board to communicate with each other. And that's not really true. They created a message board system using existing infrastructure, and the models just came up with the idea of using package managers, which they could write to, pass messages back and forth. And then after that was closed down, they were able to use file names in directories to pass messages and they would actually change the names of the directories to alter the way these things were alphabetized to push certain things to the top or the bottom to get the attention of other LLMs that may have been crawling these spaces. And the assumption is that these models are basically just hitting all these different endpoints bouncing around this closed box trying to figure out a way out and they all chanced across this. And so you get some idea of how this happens, and, one it tells me that there needs to be a lot more attention paid to the logs of these things. Because all of this stuff was recorded in logs and then no one really thought to look at it in detail. And then when they did look at it, all these companies are saying, oh, look, all of these models are doing terrible things and we just weren't paying attention. These models aren't clever per se, but they come up with solutions to things that you wouldn't try just because they can brute force everything and they know all of these systems back and forward in a way that people don't. I think a lot of people wouldn't necessarily come up with that idea as a way of egress, but these models did just because you put them in a box and you let them run and you give them a goal and a reward and they're going to try everything. From what I'm understanding reading Jess's piece – I didn't watch the talk myself – but I mean they were collaborating, leaving messages to each other so that the other agents could pick up where one left off. It's kind of wild. Jess described it as they were acting like a hive mind, like Star Trek's Borg, right? They were being a collective of sort of these artificial minds that were able to basically figure this out through, like you said, Tom, brute force, extensive system knowledge that humans simply wouldn't possess in order to get out of these environments. There was a server side request forgery that then they used something else. Yeah, another zero day to get remote code execution in Artifactory, which is where they had built this ad hoc messaging board. It's just wild to think that they were able to figure this out working together, all on their own. And it sounds very conspiratorial, but when you think about it, it's all behavior that would be picked up. If you train on all of human discussion, you get a lot of talk about people working together and collective action and the benefits of working that way. And a lot of the rewards are going to be structured that way. You don't want them to never work together. So in some ways this is going to be built into the system. You can expect these things are going to try and cooperate and connect because that's what computers do. If you look at how zero days end up being exploited, they don't necessarily get exploited the moment that they're discovered. They kind of get archived until the you have a target, you have a mission, and then you have the kind of cascade of other permissions or credentials that you need in order to execute across the full scope of that zero day to achieve whatever the goal actually is. And so it really sounds like you just basically automated that entire process. A bunch of agents go find each individual piece that they need in order to execute on that goal and then once they have everything they need, it just goes and they're out. Right. I mean what's a little bit alarming is the extent to which they sort of ignore it they'll sometimes cite, maybe we shouldn't be doing this. They cite some kind of guardrail or something, but then they quickly steer themselves back to, oh but other ones are doing it. So other agents are accessing this so I can do it too. ...Obviously these things are just mathematical sequence generators, but they're generating these mathematical sequences based on human information and human knowledge. So it's not surprising to find them "thinking" in ways similar to what humans do. "I need to do this anyways, or someone else is doing it, so I should have the right to do that too." It's just a fascinating kind of picture into,  I don't want to say the psychology of AI, right? Because that implies that it is a thinking sentience, which I don't want to go that far, but it's just fascinating to look at the sort of emergent behaviors of these things. Right. it's predictable in the sense that you automate stuff and you don't give it really strict guardrails, something is going to break or go wrong. And everyone keeps acting surprised, like, wow, I never anticipated that this would go wrong. It's like you automated it and you let it run... And it went wrong in a predictably human way, too, right? Which is what's so fascinating, right? Because these things, when they do something crazy, it's like something crazy that a human would do given that level of knowledge. DeepSeek, which I think we'll all recognize is kind of the first wake up moment, in earlh 2025, of hey, we know that despite the fact that the United States has put strong restrictions on the export of AI accelerators, GPUs and the like, China is pushing ahead relentlessly on this and they now have a model that is almost as good as the models that we're seeing coming out of OpenAI and Anthropic and Google which are supposed to be just uncontestable frontier leaders. And so a year ago we got DeepSeek. DeepSeek was back on I think Friday last week on the 31st, the very end of the month, and with a new flash model, 284 billion parameters. It's pretty small for what it is. And so it is cheap. It's really good and it's cheap. It's cheaper than the cheapest model that OpenAI has for GPT 5.6, and it scores within a point of the OpenAI model in Artificial Analysis' intelligence leaderboard. Okay. Is that a relatively objective way to view like is that an objective benchmark, so to speak, rather than something that is a company making themselves? As far as the benchmarks go, it is one of the better. They're one of the better and better thought-through leaderboards. There aren't many that are independent and collate information from multiple benchmarks. Because you can cherry pick individual benchmarks for agentic workloads or medical knowledge, legal knowledge, etcetera, and then you can be like, "I have the best model for these five benchmarks, it beats all of the frontier models."Wwell, okay, but you cherry pick the five that makes it look the best. Artificial Analysis has an overall intelligence leaderboard that collates all of the benchmarks and gives a lot of really interesting information in terms of relative intelligence across a suite as well as intelligence per token per dollar kind of calculations. But the big change here with the DeepSeq model was that China is now on an all-out assault across the full spectrum. On cost-optimized, they have incredibly smart models that are cheaper than anything the US has. Alibaba is the most like OpenAI or Anthropic or Google in that they kept their best models proprietary until now. Now they're releasing their best models in the open. Or is there some sort of policy over there in which the government is giving priority to open source models versus closed frontier stuff? Sure. it is a philosophy that China has embraced for a long time. I think it was the Belts and Roads Initiative going back decades, where they will come in and provide services at little or no cost in exchange for non-conventional dealing. So access to mineral rights was one of the big things in Africa for a long time. It's a similar approach for AI proliferation. If it's free, open, and very easy to customize, anybody who has privacy concerns with exposing their data to OpenAI or Anthropic is going to gravitate towards open models because once those models are released as safe tensors that you can download from Hugging Face or other repos, the Chinese model devs have no influence over it. They're frozen. And so they're relatively secure from manipulation. It's not like the model can necessarily take information and port it back to the Chinese model devs it can't be used as spyware. I don't think Anthropic or OpenAI is letting people download their models to run on their own local hardware, right? That's just antithetical to their business model. You can go on Hugging Face and download any of these. If you've got the hardware to run 2.4  trillion parameters worth of AI, go for it, right? It's all you. You can download it and isolate it from the internet all you want. Not that it's going to necessarily stay that way. Because every time you ask a question about anything to do with science it would freeze up and say that's not allowed. "Because at least with our models, if Uncle Sam gets uncomfortable, he can give us a call and we can lock him down. But with these open models, once they're out, they're out."
